========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, I guess… it’s mostly my eating. Like after work or class, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have dinner and get some emails done on my laptop, but then I end up eating a whole takeout meal, and then chips, sweets, whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. I don’t even really mean to, it just kind of turns into way more than I planned.

And it’s weird because I don’t think I’d ever eat like that if someone was sitting there with me. Like, at work I’m pretty normal about it—I mean, I’m in finance, so people are always at their desks with coffee and random snacks, and everyone acts busy all the time—but when I’m alone, it’s just… a lot. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone.

Usually more than once a week, not just like a random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t really weigh myself regularly because then I get kind of fixated on the number, and also my scale is shoved under a pile of laundry right now, which is probably not helpful.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I can’t tell if that’s actual weight or just, like, jeans being terrible after you wash them. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess… it’s mostly my eating. Like after work or class, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have dinner and get some emails done on my laptop, but then I end up eating a whole takeout meal, and then chips, sweets, whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. I don’t even really mean to, it just kind of turns into way more than I planned.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I don’t think I’d ever eat like that if someone was sitting there with me. Like, at work I’m pretty normal about it—I mean, I’m in finance, so people are always at their desks with coffee and random snacks, and everyone acts busy all the time—but when I’m alone, it’s just… a lot. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Sometimes I’m annoyed with myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But then I’ll also start thinking about how I should’ve just stayed later at work or gone to the gym, or how my roommate somehow manages to meal prep every Sunday, which is honestly kind of impressive because I can’t even keep cilantro from going bad in the fridge.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure what counts as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like it's hard to stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that worries me. Once I start, it’s like I’m kind of watching myself do it? Like I’ll be reaching for the next thing and in my head I’m saying, “Jaime, stop, you still have that budget report to finish,” or whatever assignment I’ve been avoiding.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m not even enjoying it anymore, which sounds so stupid when I say it out loud. It’s like when you keep scrolling through emails even though none of them matter—sorry, that’s a weird comparison—but it has that same autopilot feeling. I know I should stop, but it feels really hard to actually make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: Um… more than I wanted to admit, honestly. I tried to check because I wasn’t sure if I was exaggerating, so I looked back at my journal and like, my food delivery charges — which was depressing, because those apps make it way too easy to reorder the same thing.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started at work. I don’t really weigh myself regularly because then I get kind of fixated on the number, and also my scale is shoved under a pile of laundry right now, which is probably not helpful.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I can’t tell if that’s actual weight or just, like, jeans being terrible after you wash them. I mean, my roommate has this whole shelf of vitamins and random teas and stuff, and sometimes I don’t even know what half of it is, but no, that’s not something I use after eating. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. Like after work or class, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have dinner and get some emails done on my laptop, but then I end up eating a whole takeout meal, and then chips, sweets, whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. I don’t even really mean to, it just kind of turns into way more than I planned.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I don’t think I’d ever eat like that if someone was sitting there with me. Like, at work I’m pretty normal about it—I mean, I’m in finance, so people are always at their desks with coffee and random snacks, and everyone acts busy all the time—but when I’m alone, it’s just… a lot. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Sometimes I’m annoyed with myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But then I’ll also start thinking about how I should’ve just stayed later at work or gone to the gym, or how my roommate somehow manages to meal prep every Sunday, which is honestly kind of impressive because I can’t even keep cilantro from going bad in the fridge.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure what counts as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like it's hard to stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that worries me. Once I start, it’s like I’m kind of watching myself do it? Like I’ll be reaching for the next thing and in my head I’m saying, “Jaime, stop, you still have that budget report to finish,” or whatever assignment I’ve been avoiding.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m not even enjoying it anymore, which sounds so stupid when I say it out loud. It’s like when you keep scrolling through emails even though none of them matter—sorry, that’s a weird comparison—but it has that same autopilot feeling. I know I should stop, but it feels really hard to actually make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: Um… more than I wanted to admit, honestly. I tried to check because I wasn’t sure if I was exaggerating, so I looked back at my journal and like, my food delivery charges — which was depressing, because those apps make it way too easy to reorder the same thing.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started at work. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t really weigh myself regularly because then I get kind of fixated on the number, and also my scale is shoved under a pile of laundry right now, which is probably not helpful.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I can’t tell if that’s actual weight or just, like, jeans being terrible after you wash them. So… I haven’t noticed anything I’d confidently call significant, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not in any planned way. I’ve definitely felt really gross afterward sometimes, like too full and just kind of mad at myself, but I haven’t been, like, doing something afterward on purpose.\n\nLaxatives… no, I don’t really keep those around. I mean, my roommate has this whole shelf of vitamins and random teas and stuff, and sometimes I don’t even know what half of it is, but no, that’s not something I use after eating. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I tried to check because I wasn’t sure if I was exaggerating, so I looked back at my journal and like, my food delivery charges — which was depressing, because those apps make it way too easy to reorder the same thing.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started at work. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t really weigh myself regularly because then I get kind of fixated on the number, and also my scale is shoved under a pile of laundry right now, which is probably not helpful.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I can’t tell if that’s actual weight or just, like, jeans being terrible after you wash them. So… I haven’t noticed anything I’d confidently call significant, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not in any planned way. I’ve definitely felt really gross afterward sometimes, like too full and just kind of mad at myself, but I haven’t been, like, doing something afterward on purpose.\n\nLaxatives… no, I don’t really keep those around. I mean, my roommate has this whole shelf of vitamins and random teas and stuff, and sometimes I don’t even know what half of it is, but no, that’s not something I use after eating. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’ve definitely felt really gross afterward sometimes, like too full and just kind of mad at myself, but I haven’t been, like, doing something afterward on purpose.\n\nLaxatives… no, I don’t really keep those around. I mean, my roommate has this whole shelf of vitamins and random teas and stuff, and sometimes I don’t even know what half of it is, but no, that’s not something I use after eating. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food alone, or are there other people around when it happens?\n\nPatient: Usually alone, yeah. I kind of… plan it around that, I guess. Like I’ll wait until my roommate is gone or if I’m at home, until my family’s gone to bed.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. During the day I’m always trying to seem like I have everything together, and I’ll talk about being healthy or make normal choices in front of people, and then I don’t want anyone seeing how much I can actually eat later. So I try to make sure nobody’s around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food alone, or are there other people around when it happens?\n\nPatient: Usually alone, yeah. I kind of… plan it around that, I guess. Like I’ll wait until my roommate is gone or if I’m at home, until my family’s gone to bed.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. During the day I’m always trying to seem like I have everything together, and I’ll talk about being healthy or make normal choices in front of people, and then I don’t want anyone seeing how much I can actually eat later. So I try to make sure nobody’s around.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you planned been occurring at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. I know I kind of said that already, but I did look back because I didn’t trust my memory. My journal and the food delivery charges made it pretty clear it’s been happening most weeks since the work restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random night here and there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess… it’s mostly my eating. Like after work or class, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have dinner and get some emails done on my laptop, but then I end up eating a whole takeout meal, and then chips, sweets, whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. Sometimes I’m annoyed with myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But then I’ll also start thinking about how I should’ve just stayed later at work or gone to the gym, or how my roommate somehow manages to meal prep every Sunday, which is honestly kind of impressive because I can’t even keep cilantro from going bad in the fridge.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure what counts as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like it's hard to stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that worries me. So… I haven’t noticed anything I’d confidently call significant, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not in any planned way. I’ve definitely felt really gross afterward sometimes, like too full and just kind of mad at myself, but I haven’t been, like, doing something afterward on purpose.\n\nLaxatives… no, I don’t really keep those around. I mean, my roommate has this whole shelf of vitamins and random teas and stuff, and sometimes I don’t even know what half of it is, but no, that’s not something I use after eating. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food alone, or are there other people around when it happens?\n\nPatient: Usually alone, yeah. I kind of… plan it around that, I guess. Like I’ll wait until my roommate is gone or if I’m at home, until my family’s gone to bed.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. During the day I’m always trying to seem like I have everything together, and I’ll talk about being healthy or make normal choices in front of people, and then I don’t want anyone seeing how much I can actually eat later. Sometimes I’m annoyed with myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But then I’ll also start thinking about how I should’ve just stayed later at work or gone to the gym, or how my roommate somehow manages to meal prep every Sunday, which is honestly kind of impressive because I can’t even keep cilantro from going bad in the fridge.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure what counts as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like it's hard to stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that worries me. Once I start, it’s like I’m kind of watching myself do it? Like I’ll be reaching for the next thing and in my head I’m saying, “Jaime, stop, you still have that budget report to finish,” or whatever assignment I’ve been avoiding.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m not even enjoying it anymore, which sounds so stupid when I say it out loud. It’s like when you keep scrolling through emails even though none of them matter—sorry, that’s a weird comparison—but it has that same autopilot feeling. I know I should stop, but it feels really hard to actually make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: Um… more than I wanted to admit, honestly. I tried to check because I wasn’t sure if I was exaggerating, so I looked back at my journal and like, my food delivery charges — which was depressing, because those apps make it way too easy to reorder the same thing.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started at work. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t really weigh myself regularly because then I get kind of fixated on the number, and also my scale is shoved under a pile of laundry right now, which is probably not helpful.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I can’t tell if that’s actual weight or just, like, jeans being terrible after you wash them. So… I haven’t noticed anything I’d confidently call significant, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not in any planned way. I’ve definitely felt really gross afterward sometimes, like too full and just kind of mad at myself, but I haven’t been, like, doing something afterward on purpose.\n\nLaxatives… no, I don’t really keep those around. I mean, my roommate has this whole shelf of vitamins and random teas and stuff, and sometimes I don’t even know what half of it is, but no, that’s not something I use after eating. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food alone, or are there other people around when it happens?\n\nPatient: Usually alone, yeah. I kind of… plan it around that, I guess. Like I’ll wait until my roommate is gone or if I’m at home, until my family’s gone to bed.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. During the day I’m always trying to seem like I have everything together, and I’ll talk about being healthy or make normal choices in front of people, and then I don’t want anyone seeing how much I can actually eat later. So I try to make sure nobody’s around.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you planned been occurring at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. I know I kind of said that already, but I did look back because I didn’t trust my memory. My journal and the food delivery charges made it pretty clear it’s been happening most weeks since the work restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most. Once I start, it feels like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while I’m thinking, like, Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish and you’re not even enjoying this anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted thinking about the report, or how behind I am, but my hand is still just… getting more food. So, yes, it does feel like I can’t really control it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes where you consume a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I know I kind of already said that, but… it’s like once I start, I’m not really deciding anymore. I’ll be thinking, Jaime, stop, you still have that budget report to finish, or I’ll tell myself I’m not even enjoying it, and then somehow I’m still reaching for the next thing.\n\nIt’s weird because with work stuff I’m usually pretty controlled—like spreadsheets, deadlines, all of that—and then with this it’s like my brain is off somewhere else and my hand just keeps going. So yes, I feel like I can’t control it in those moments.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food alone, or are there other people around when it happens?\n\nPatient: Usually alone, yeah. I kind of… plan it around that, I guess. Like I’ll wait until my roommate is gone or if I’m at home, until my family’s gone to bed.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. During the day I’m always trying to seem like I have everything together, and I’ll talk about being healthy or make normal choices in front of people, and then I don’t want anyone seeing how much I can actually eat later. So I try to make sure nobody’s around.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you planned been occurring at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. I know I kind of said that already, but I did look back because I didn’t trust my memory. My journal and the food delivery charges made it pretty clear it’s been happening most weeks since the work restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most. Once I start, it feels like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while I’m thinking, like, Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish and you’re not even enjoying this anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted thinking about the report, or how behind I am, but my hand is still just… getting more food. So, yes, it does feel like I can’t really control it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes where you consume a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I know I kind of already said that, but… it’s like once I start, I’m not really deciding anymore. I’ll be thinking, Jaime, stop, you still have that budget report to finish, or I’ll tell myself I’m not even enjoying it, and then somehow I’m still reaching for the next thing.\n\nIt’s weird because with work stuff I’m usually pretty controlled—like spreadsheets, deadlines, all of that—and then with this it’s like my brain is off somewhere else and my hand just keeps going. So yes, I feel like I can’t control it in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used excessive exercise to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I’ve had times where I thought, “Okay, I should go for a walk,” but then I get caught up in something else or I’m just tired. And honestly, exercise has always been kind of inconsistent for me—like I’ll download an app and do it for three days, then forget it exists.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve really done that after eating episodes. Not in some intense way to burn it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever fasted or skipped meals to try to make up for the calories you consumed during one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I don’t think I’ve done it on purpose like, “I’m fasting to make up for that.” Sometimes I skip breakfast, but that’s also because mornings are chaotic and I’ll be late or I’ll get caught up checking emails, and then suddenly it’s noon.\n\nI mean, after one of those episodes I might think, “Ugh, I don’t want to eat for a while,” but I don’t know if that counts as trying to make up for calories. I mean… I don’t know. Sometimes I feel kind of gross or annoyed with myself afterward, like, “Why did I do that?” But I’m not sure if I’d call it significant distress or anxiety.

It’s more like I get frustrated, and then I try to distract myself—usually with work stuff, or I’ll start reorganizing something random, like my email folders. Which, honestly, I do that when I’m stressed about deadlines too, so it’s hard to tell what’s from the eating and what’s just me being… me.

So yeah, I guess I feel bad after, but I don’t know how big of a deal it is compared to other stress.

Usually more than once a week, not just like a random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t really weigh myself regularly because then I get kind of fixated on the number, and also my scale is shoved under a pile of laundry right now, which is probably not helpful.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I can’t tell if that’s actual weight or just, like, jeans being terrible after you wash them. So… I haven’t noticed anything I’d confidently call significant, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food alone, or are there other people around when it happens?\n\nPatient: Usually alone, yeah. I kind of… plan it around that, I guess. Like I’ll wait until my roommate is gone or if I’m at home, until my family’s gone to bed.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. During the day I’m always trying to seem like I have everything together, and I’ll talk about being healthy or make normal choices in front of people, and then I don’t want anyone seeing how much I can actually eat later. So I try to make sure nobody’s around.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you planned been occurring at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. I know I kind of said that already, but I did look back because I didn’t trust my memory. My journal and the food delivery charges made it pretty clear it’s been happening most weeks since the work restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most. I don’t think so… not in any clear way. Like, I don’t make myself do something afterward to “cancel it out,” if that’s what you mean.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess… it’s mostly my eating. Like after work or class, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have dinner and get some emails done on my laptop, but then I end up eating a whole takeout meal, and then chips, sweets, whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. I don’t even really mean to, it just kind of turns into way more than I planned.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I don’t think I’d ever eat like that if someone was sitting there with me. Like, at work I’m pretty normal about it—I mean, I’m in finance, so people are always at their desks with coffee and random snacks, and everyone acts busy all the time—but when I’m alone, it’s just… a lot. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Sometimes I’m annoyed with myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But then I’ll also start thinking about how I should’ve just stayed later at work or gone to the gym, or how my roommate somehow manages to meal prep every Sunday, which is honestly kind of impressive because I can’t even keep cilantro from going bad in the fridge.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe a little, but I’m not totally sure what counts as guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like it's hard to stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that worries me. Once I start, it’s like I’m kind of watching myself do it? Like I’ll be reaching for the next thing and in my head I’m saying, “Jaime, stop, you still have that budget report to finish,” or whatever assignment I’ve been avoiding.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m not even enjoying it anymore, which sounds so stupid when I say it out loud. It’s like when you keep scrolling through emails even though none of them matter—sorry, that’s a weird comparison—but it has that same autopilot feeling. I know I should stop, but it feels really hard to actually make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: Um… more than I wanted to admit, honestly. I tried to check because I wasn’t sure if I was exaggerating, so I looked back at my journal and like, my food delivery charges — which was depressing, because those apps make it way too easy to reorder the same thing.\n\nBut yeah, it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started at work. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t really weigh myself regularly because then I get kind of fixated on the number, and also my scale is shoved under a pile of laundry right now, which is probably not helpful.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I can’t tell if that’s actual weight or just, like, jeans being terrible after you wash them. So… I haven’t noticed anything I’d confidently call significant, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Like, not in any planned way. I’ve definitely felt really gross afterward sometimes, like too full and just kind of mad at myself, but I haven’t been, like, doing something afterward on purpose.\n\nLaxatives… no, I don’t really keep those around. I mean, my roommate has this whole shelf of vitamins and random teas and stuff, and sometimes I don’t even know what half of it is, but no, that’s not something I use after eating. And making myself throw up… no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there have been times when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, and afterwards, you didn't do anything to try to undo the effects of eating that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. It’ll be after work or after class, and I’ll sit down with my laptop “just for dinner,” and then somehow it’s a whole takeout meal, plus chips, something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer.\n\nAnd I don’t do anything after to, like, cancel it out or anything. I just kind of sit there feeling awful and embarrassed. It’s way more than I meant to eat, and honestly way more than I’d ever eat if someone else was around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time, felt a lack of control, and didn't try to undo the effects of eating afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s exactly what it feels like, I think. Like once I start, it’s not even… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m having this great time eating. It’s more like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while part of me is thinking, “Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish,” or whatever thing I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I’m not even enjoying it anymore, but I keep going. I don’t really do anything afterward to undo it. I just sit there feeling kind of stuck and gross about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself regularly, honestly. I kind of avoid the scale because then I’ll start thinking about it all day, and then I’m late for class or I’m messing around with my work clothes trying to figure out what fits right.\n\nLike, some days my jeans feel tighter, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight or just, you know, laundry shrinking them or me being bloated or whatever. So… noticeable? Not like a number I can give you. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these episodes of eating more than you planned happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I actually tried to check because my memory gets kind of fuzzy with weeks blending together, especially since everything got reorganized at work and my schedule’s been weird.\n\nI looked back through my journal and my food delivery charges, and it’s been happening most weeks since the restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, honestly. Not just, like, one random bad night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly every time. Sometimes I mostly feel annoyed at myself, like I wasted time and now the kitchen’s a mess, and then I start thinking about how I still need to answer emails or finish whatever I was supposed to be doing.\n\nBut, yeah, I guess there’s this embarrassed feeling too. I don’t usually want anyone to know it happened. It’s hard to explain without making it sound dramatic. I just kind of feel bad afterward and want to move on, but then I keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these large amounts of food alone, or are there other people around when it happens?\n\nPatient: Usually alone, yeah. I kind of… plan it around that, I guess. Like I’ll wait until my roommate is gone or if I’m at home, until my family’s gone to bed.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. During the day I’m always trying to seem like I have everything together, and I’ll talk about being healthy or make normal choices in front of people, and then I don’t want anyone seeing how much I can actually eat later. So I try to make sure nobody’s around.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating more than you planned been occurring at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. I know I kind of said that already, but I did look back because I didn’t trust my memory. My journal and the food delivery charges made it pretty clear it’s been happening most weeks since the work restructuring started. Usually more than once a week, not just like a random night here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most. Once I start, it feels like I’m watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, even while I’m thinking, like, Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish and you’re not even enjoying this anymore.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted thinking about the report, or how behind I am, but my hand is still just… getting more food. So, yes, it does feel like I can’t really control it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes where you consume a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I know I kind of already said that, but… it’s like once I start, I’m not really deciding anymore. I’ll be thinking, Jaime, stop, you still have that budget report to finish, or I’ll tell myself I’m not even enjoying it, and then somehow I’m still reaching for the next thing.\n\nIt’s weird because with work stuff I’m usually pretty controlled—like spreadsheets, deadlines, all of that—and then with this it’s like my brain is off somewhere else and my hand just keeps going. So yes, I feel like I can’t control it in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used excessive exercise to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive, exactly. I mean, I’ve had times where I thought, “Okay, I should go for a walk,” but then I get caught up in something else or I’m just tired. And honestly, exercise has always been kind of inconsistent for me—like I’ll download an app and do it for three days, then forget it exists.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve really done that after eating episodes. Not in some intense way to burn it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever fasted or skipped meals to try to make up for the calories you consumed during one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I don’t think I’ve done it on purpose like, “I’m fasting to make up for that.” Sometimes I skip breakfast, but that’s also because mornings are chaotic and I’ll be late or I’ll get caught up checking emails, and then suddenly it’s noon.\n\nI mean, after one of those episodes I might think, “Ugh, I don’t want to eat for a while,” but I don’t know if that counts as trying to make up for calories.
